structured reflection activity conducted throughout the year that helps Campus Leads analyze their community, identify challenges and opportunities, and develop strategies to build healthier campus communities.","Building a campus community is an iterative process. Throughout the year, Campus Leads gain new experiences, face different challenges, and discover what works best for their campus. The Retro Program creates recurring opportunities to pause, reflect, and learn from these experiences rather than simply moving from one initiative to the next.\n\nBy bringing Campus Leads together to collectively reflect on their communities, the activity helps uncover common patterns across campuses, generate new ideas, and develop practical strategies that can be applied throughout the year. Revisiting these reflections in later camps also enables Campus Leads to measure their progress, understand what has changed, and adapt their approach based on real experiences.","Phase 1: Onboarding Camp – Understanding the Community\n\nThe first Retro is conducted during the Onboarding Camp, where Campus Leads are divided into small groups.\n\nEach group reflects on their campus using the Wind, Anchor & Island framework:\n\n🌬️ Winds – What is helping your community grow? What strengths, opportunities, or support systems are moving the campus forward?\n⚓ Anchors – What is holding the community back? What challenges, blockers, or recurring issues exist?\n🏝️ Islands – What does your ideal campus community look like? What kind of culture, activities, and impact do you want to create?\n\nOnce the groups have mapped their observations, they move into a solutioning session. Together, they identify practical ways to overcome their Anchors, strengthen their Winds, and move closer to their Islands. The ideas are documented visually using charts, sticky notes, sketches, or canvases before being presented to the larger group.\n\nThe solutions developed during this session become ideas that Campus Leads can take back and experiment with in their own communities.\n\n\nPhase 2: Winter Camp – Reflecting on the Journey\n\nDuring the Winter Camp, Campus Leads revisit the reflections, challenges, and action plans they created during the Onboarding Camp. Having spent several months building their communities, they now reflect on what actually happened in their campuses.\n\nThe discussion focuses on:\n\nWhat worked well?\nWhat didn't work as expected?\nWhich challenges were overcome, and which still remain?\nWhat could have been done differently?\nWhat should be improved during the rest of the journey?\n\nAlongside these reflections, the Foundation Campus Team shares community data, patterns, and observations collected over the past few months. These discussions help Campus Leads connect their own experiences with broader trends across the network.\n\nFor many Campus Leads, this is also the point where several concepts introduced during the Onboarding Camp begin to make sense. They understand why recurring community meetups, consistent engagement, ownership, and other community-building practices matter—not because they were told to do them, but because they have now experienced their impact firsthand.\n\nThe session concludes with Campus Leads identifying new ideas, refined strategies, and actionable improvements that they can take back to their campuses for the remainder of the academic year.",{"items":125},[126,127,128,129,130],"Campus Leads develop a deeper understanding of their community.","Common challenges and opportunities across campuses are As a result, programs often focus on execution without enough thought being given to who they are for, the experience they create, or the impact they should have.\n\nThe Program Design Sprint helps Campus Leads adopt a more intentional approach to designing programs. Participants begin by understanding their campus, validating assumptions through real conversations with students, and identifying the needs they want to address.\n\nUsing these insights, Campus Leads collaboratively design programs with clear outcomes that encourage meaningful participation and strengthen the community. Through structured design frameworks and iterative feedback, the workshop equips participants with a repeatable process for designing better community programs.","Module 1: Audience Discovery\n\nBefore designing a program, participants first understand who they are designing for.\n\nWorking in campus teams, participants reflect on:\n\nTheir target audience.\nExisting strengths and opportunities within their campus.\nActive makers and the current state of their community.\nThe goals they want their community to achieve.\n\nThese assumptions are then validated through a Cold Call activity. Participants speak with students outside their Core Team to understand what they genuinely want to learn, what prevents them from joining communities, and what kinds of experiences they wish existed on campus.\n\nRather than designing based on assumptions, the insights gathered from these conversations become the foundation for the programs that follow.\n\nModule 2: Program Design\n\nUsing the insights gathered during Audience Discovery, teams design a complete community program using the Program Canvas.\n\nThe canvas helps participants define:\n\nThe target audience.\nThe value proposition of the program.\nHow participants build connections and a sense of belonging.\nPotential bottlenecks and expected outcomes.\n\nOnce the canvas is complete, teams participate in a Shark Tank style peer review, where another group evaluates the design, asks critical questions, and suggests improvements. Facilitators guide discussions throughout the session, helping teams refine their ideas into stronger, more meaningful programs.\n\nModule 3: Execution & Continuity\n\nThe final module focuses on transforming a good program idea into an executable and sustainable community initiative.\n\nParticipants discuss common execution challenges, adapt planning checklists, improve how their programs are positioned and marketed, and design ways to keep participants engaged after the event. The session emphasizes that successful programs do not end when the event is over—they should create pathways for continued participation within the community.\n\nEach team leaves with a validated Participants experience how individual choices can shape the success of both their own team and the larger community.","Leadership often involves making decisions where personal gain and collective good do not always align. While collaboration is easy to encourage, it becomes difficult when competition and incentives influence our choices.\n\nThe Token Game creates a safe environment for participants to experience these situations firsthand. As the game progresses, teams negotiate, build trust, break agreements, and respond to the decisions of others. Through reflection, participants begin to understand how leadership, governance, and communities are shaped not just by individual success, but by the choices people make together.","1. Form Teams\n\nParticipants are divided into teams, with each team selecting a leader responsible for representing the group during discussions.\n\n2. Play the Rounds\n\nAcross multiple rounds, every team privately chooses between two tokens. The combination of choices determines how points are distributed, creating situations where cooperation and competition continuously influence one another.\n\n3. Leadership Discussions\n\nAt key points during the game, team leaders come together to negotiate, discuss strategies, and build agreements before returning to their teams. Participants experience collaboration, conflict, trust, and betrayal as the game evolves.\n\n4. Reflection\n\nThe activity concludes with a facilitated discussion where participants reflect on their decisions, the behaviour of different teams, and the overall outcome. The conversation connects these experiences to leadership, community building, governance, and the importance of creating systems where everyone can grow
